Fix: ten to twelve development shapes with one pair of runs each, and a fresh set once the first has been used for a choice between versions; prints cost paper, not robot hours.

### To keep

1. Driving the exam case on the robot with the models trained without a sheet (block B); it should grow, not shrink.
2. Rules, controls and alternating order fixed before each session, and the plain sentence that four runs per setting can only show large differences (lines 169 to 170).
3. Scoring a run from its pictures afterwards, outside the driver, and checking the measure against a photo with a ruler before it is used to accept or reject anything (block E).
4. Section 8 as it stands: no new hand-written steering, no larger twist factor as the fix, no switch of model as the fix, no higher stop threshold, the camera not touched, nothing judged on the six sheets alone.
5. More different shapes before more episodes of the same sheets (report 8.2), each new shape printed solid and dashed, with bends as tight as the course sheets; the practice tracks redrawn before anything is printed (section 2.4).
6. One written demonstration style for both operators, tried by hand for half an hour first; a first batch of ten line ends with the number shown before more are recorded; recoveries recorded on purpose.
7. Models judged on shapes they were not trained on, trained twice, by a rule fixed before the result; retraining from scratch on everything.
8. The driver frozen before corrections are recorded, and a second correction round only if the first one helped.
9. The pictures of the ten runs of 11.11 kept as a fixed test that is never trained on (as a report, not as a pass rule for the walk).
10. Every statement marked measured, calculated, guess or judgement; the list of causes ruled out (section 2.3) and the corrections to earlier statements (section 2.4).
11. Nothing on the robot without Emranul's go and a written checklist; decisions put to Emranul one at a time; robot work ends 20.12.26, freeze 8.1.27.
12. Asking the professor in writing now instead of waiting for the ten-minute meeting of 23.11.26.

## Is this the right imitation-learning plan for an unseen line? (folder `method`)

**The critic's verdict.** Partly sound. Under the method lens the plan has the right order (driver test, then more different tracks in one written style, then retraining judged on unseen tracks, then corrections), and its biggest bet is backed by numbers I measured from the project's own files: the 23 fold trainings of 9 to 11.11.26 all give back only 0.42 to 0.60 of the demonstrated net turn on sheets they never saw, whatever the recipe, so the lever is the data and not another recipe (m07); and where the camera looks along the line the models give back 0.59 of the turn, against 0.20 to 0.28 where it does not (m06). Three things would waste the 10 to 12 robot hours as written. (1) The demonstration rules are not yet rules a one-picture model can follow everywhere: with the sheets laid as recorded the camera cannot look along a quarter of the way (25.0 % of rows, 120 of 209 episodes, m02), the plan has no lay-out rule for recording and no rule for that regime, the loop rule has no trigger the picture shows well, and nothing settles what happens to the 209 old episodes, which will still be 80 % of the pictures. (2) The judging rule of step 4 (5 points of path within 45 degrees) is blind to what failed: it reads 0.960 at the first bend of sheet 03, where the robot lost the puck, while the same model gives back 0.52 of the turn there (m05); it must be replaced by a small panel fixed in advance (net turn given back, whole-episode stop, a path guard, stress tests). (3) The model comes into the loop too late and the stop has no remedy: no pilot of the new style before the hours are spent, no model attempt on the fresh prints, a correction tool that does not exist yet with one round in the last robot week, 58 to 78 new stop pictures against 71 of 209 episodes that never stop, and short recoveries that would add false stop pictures. Where my evidence is: scripts m01 to m07 with their printed outputs (.out.txt) in D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/ . m01 is the earlier attempt's script; I ran it again and its output is identical to the character. m03 part A is a least-squares read-out on features saved on 9.11.26 (closed form, 40 seconds on the processor), not a training run. Nothing was connected to the robot, no git command was run that changes anything, no existing file was edited.

**The checker's overall verdict.** All 14 objections point at real gaps in the plan and none has to be thrown out, but only three stand as written (P5, P12, P13); the other eleven are right in direction and need their size or their fix changed. The critic's evidence is sound where I reran it: m02, m05 and m06 give output identical to the saved files, and my own script gives the same values as m04 and m07 [measured]. My scripts and outputs are in D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/check/ (c01 to c04, each with its .out.txt); nothing was trained, nothing touched the robot, no git command changed anything, no existing file was edited. The biggest correction concerns the critic's central number, 'the share of the demonstrated net turn the model gives back' (about one half). It carries the verdict's first claim, the panel of P2, the pilot rule of P7, the loop rule of P9 and the reading in P10. It is below one for any honest but imperfect model, because the windows are picked by what the demonstration did. Picked instead by what the model asks (30 degrees or more over ten rows), the demonstrations show 1.00 (model E) and 0.94 (model A) of the asked turn; multiplying every twist by 1.5 lifts the share from 0.51 to 0.77 while the error of the window's turn grows from 28 to 32 degrees [measured, c01]. So the model is not biased towards small turns. It is unsure where the sharp turns come (its summed twist follows the demonstrated one with a correlation of 0.71), and a keep rule built on the share would reward bigger twists, not better ones. 'Twenty-three trainings did not move it, so the lever is the data' does not follow either: all 23 are one family (one picture, the same kind of twist output) and across them the share follows the mean size of the twist (correlation +0.92). Other sizes shrink on checking. By the check's own reach table the camera cannot look along 22 % of the recorded way, not 25 %. The critic's lay-out rule 0 lowers the stretch beyond the camera's reach on the six sheets from 20.0 % to 15.7 % of the line [calculated, c04], not from 15 % to 5 %; that figure belongs to another threshold and another placement rule. Of the 71 episodes that 'never stop', 24 fire on the last picture, where the recording ends and no second picture can confirm. Rule 3 clashes with old rows in which the camera had room on 5 to 7 % of the rows, so 'two styles averaged' is a smaller risk there than the quoted 32 % suggests. Several fixes cannot be read with the runs they name (0.59 on 8 pilot episodes, 3 mm on three stops, 6 paired runs), one contradicts the critic's own keep list (48 near-end starts 'in place of' the batch of 10), one clashes with Emranul's limit rule ('turn the forearm as far as it goes'), and the keep list's 'rule 3 has the project's numbers behind it' reads too much into a comparison of 'inside the camera's reach' against 'beyond it'. What survives and should go into the plan: a lay-out rule for recording and a written rule for the stretches the camera cannot follow; the 5-point path rule replaced by a small panel fixed in advance; per-shape sampling and a decision about the old episodes fixed before any result; no stop labels in the middle of a line, and a stop candidate; the take-over tool with an owner in step 1; hand corrections during the replay instead of recording again; a pilot and the model on fresh prints from the first recording day; 9 to 10 honest robot hours for the new shapes; the wrist held by the replay; pass rules for the 11.11 pictures.

### Objections

**P1** (high). Where: Step 3, rules 1 and 3; the 'two lay-outs' of the new shapes; risks table, row 'Loops stay unreliable'

- Problem: Rule 3 assumes the camera can always be turned to look along the line. With the sheets laid as they were recorded it cannot on a quarter of the way. The plan has no lay-out rule for recording and no written rule for the stretches where the camera cannot follow. Those stretches are exactly where the models fail to carry over to an unseen sheet.
- Evidence: Measured, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m02_rule_3_forced_or_chosen.out.txt: 25.0 % of the 11,419 rows with a known puck direction run more than 108 degrees from 'straight away from the base', beyond what joint 4 at 117 degrees lets the camera reach (the check gives 106 to 123 degrees); 120 of 209 episodes hold such a stretch of 10 rows or more. Of 9,084 pushing rows 23.4 % are pushes more than 45 degrees off the picture's top that the limit forces; only 3.0 % are such pushes by choice. In the forced rows |joint 4| has a median of 65 degrees, so the demonstrators did not use the room they had. Measured, same folder, m06_is_the_style_learnable.out.txt (each sheet answered by the model that never saw it): where the puck travels within 20 degrees of the picture's top model E's direction is within 45 degrees in 78.4 %, its twist follows the shown twist with a correlation of 0.62 and it gives back 0.59 of the net turn; 45 to 90 degrees off: 59.0 %, 0.22 and 0.20; beyond 90: 64.9 %, 0.30 and 0.28. Model A is alike and the gap holds inside each of the six sheets. This compares different places on the sheets, it is not an experiment. Calculated by the orientation analyst (orientation_coverage/05_recording_plan.out.txt; not recomputed by the verifier or by me; I only added up its table): with the best placement of each shape end the stretch running more than 140 degrees from straight away falls from 109 of 747 cm (15 %) to 51 of 982 cm (5 %), and to 0 cm on sheets 01 to 03.
- The critic's fix: Add a rule 0 and use it for every new episode and at the exam: 'Lay the sheet so that as little of the line as possible runs back towards the robot. Among the placements that start within about 60 degrees of straight away, take the one with the fewest centimetres beyond 110 degrees. The forearm starts turned along the line.' The placement for each printed track comes from the existing calculator (orientation_coverage/05_recording_plan.py). Write the second regime down as well and try it in the hand trial: 'where the line runs back, turn the forearm as far as it goes on that side and push with the camera lagging, always the same way.' Log for each episode how many centimetres ran beyond the camera's reach. Cost: 0.5 code day, no extra robot time (block D already tests the turned start), no GPU run.
- Check: **partly**. Reran m02: output identical [measured]. With the critic's flat limit of 108 degrees 25.0 % of the 11,419 rows lie beyond the camera's reach; with the limit the check calculated per reach (106 / 112 / 116 / 123 degrees at 12 / 25 / 35 / 45 cm, orientation claim 7) it is 22.3 %, in 115 of 209 episodes (c03 part A) [measured]. The check supports the finding on its own (orientation claim 8: on paths beyond 135 degrees the demonstrators looked along the line in 0.7 % of rows, joint 4 at a median of 71). m06's gap holds, and it is a gap between 'inside reach' and 'beyond the limit': of 326 windows with the puck's travel 45 degrees or more off the picture's top, 277 are forced by the limit and only 30 are free (5 of them sharp). The model's summed twist follows the demonstrated one with a correlation of 0.75 inside reach and 0.37 beyond, inside every sheet (c03 part B) [measured]. The plan has no lay-out rule for recording [read]. So the objection stands, a little smaller. The fix is weaker than stated. The quoted gain (15 % to 5 %) is for the stretch beyond 140 degrees with the analyst's 'gentlest' placement. By the critic's own measure (beyond 110 degrees) that placement gives 20.6 % on the six sheets against 20.0 % as recorded; rule 0 as worded gives 15.7 % (88 of 562 cm; practice tracks 27.1 % to 24.0 %) (c04 part A, from orientation_coverage/05_candidates.csv) [calculated; the analyst's geometry not recomputed]. So about three quarters of the hard stretch stays whatever the placement. Rule 0 also needs the calculator and a traced line, which do not exist for an unseen sheet on the exam day. 'Turn the forearm as far as it goes' clashes with rule 7 (software limit 117, 2 to 3 degrees inside the hardware limit; a guiding hand feels only the hard stop; the old recordings reached 119.35 and 123 pictures lie beyond 117) and with what the old rows show there (median joint 4 of 65 degrees).
- The checker's fix: Keep the objection, change the fix. (1) A lay-out rule a person can apply by eye in a minute, because it must also serve on the exam day: 'turn the sheet so that the line starts within about 60 degrees of straight away and a loop or hairpin opens away from the robot'. Check the wording on the six sheets and the new prints with the calculator rerun for 'centimetres beyond 110 degrees', and write its real gain into the plan (about a fifth to a quarter less, not two thirds). (2) Record about three quarters of the new episodes by this rule and one quarter in other lay-outs on purpose, labelled, so that Emranul's wish 'any orientation' is measured and not assumed. (3) For the stretch the camera cannot follow, do not write 'as far as it goes'. Try two written ways in the hand trial on one loop print, three times each, with joint 4 shown live and a warning at 110 degrees; keep the way whose repeats agree best (spread of joint 4 at the same place). Log per episode the centimetres beyond reach. (4) Put the limit to Emranul as one decision in plain words: the camera can turn about 110 degrees to each side, so a line that runs back at the robot is always pushed half blind. Cost: 0.5 to 1 code day, 30 minutes more in the hand trial, no GPU run.

**P2** (high). Where: Step 4, second bullet: 'the report's rules: 5 points of path within 45 degrees for a setting, with both seeds'

- Problem: The measure this rule uses did not see the failures of 11.11.26 and cannot see what the plan's own candidates change. A candidate that repairs the turning or the stop would be thrown out, and one that does not could pass by luck.
- Evidence: Measured, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m05_what_the_offline_rule_sees.out.txt (my path within 45 equals the run's metrics.json to 0.001 on all six sheets): at the first bend of sheet 03 (8 standard starts, 76 rows, the model that never saw sheet 03) path within 45 degrees is 0.960, while the model gives back 0.52 of the shown turn and a fixed 1 cm step gives 0.37 of the shown turn per cm. The robot lost the puck there in runs 5 and 9. Per held-out sheet path within 45 spans 0.780 to 0.864 while the net turn given back spans 0.14 to 0.63 and right stops 21 to 75 %; sheet 05 is third best on the path (0.811) with 0.23 of the turn and 41 % of episodes never stopping. Measured, same folder, m07_seed_spread_of_the_turn_measure.out.txt: over the 23 fold trainings in 04_runs/gpu_ki014 the net turn given back stays between 0.421 and 0.597, and two seeds of one recipe differ by 0.002 to 0.073 (9 pairs), so this number is steady enough to carry a rule. Reasoning: 'which way / how far' changes only the length, and the augmentations aim at dashes, the hoodie and the camera's shift; neither can move a direction score on clean demonstration pictures by 5 points (switching augmentation off changed it by under 1 point, report 9.1 item 1a).
- The critic's fix: Replace the rule by a small panel, written down with today's values before the first run: (1) net turn given back in windows of 10 rows with 30 degrees or more, on left-out shapes: 0.51 to 0.54 today; keep a candidate that adds 0.08 or more with both seeds (above the largest seed difference seen). (2) Whole-episode stop under the fixed rule: 106 right, 71 never, 32 early of 209 today; keep at 15 more right stops with both seeds and no more early stops (the report's own rule, 9.1 item 8). (3) Guard: path within 45 degrees not down by more than 2 points with either seed. (4) Robustness candidates (dashes, shift, patches) are kept when they pass their own stress test (the walk on dashed copies of run 7's pictures; run 4's stop pictures with the hoodie painted over) and lose under 2 points on clean pictures. (5) The new shapes are reported apart from the six sheets. Cost: 0.5 to 1 code day (m05 and m07 are a start), no robot, no GPU run.
- Check: **partly**. Reran m05: identical [measured]. The rule is rule 2 of the report's section 6.9 [read]. Path within 45 degrees scores the tip's direction only, so it cannot see the twist or the stop. At the first bend of sheet 03 it reads 0.960 on 50 scored rows, where runs 5 and 9 lost the puck; switching augmentation off moved it by under 1 point (0.787 and 0.772 against 0.778 and 0.775, m07) [measured]. So the robustness candidates could never pass it: the objection stands. The replacement's first item does not. The share of the net turn is not a measure of turning. For model E it equals the squared correlation between asked and shown turn over ten rows (correlation 0.706, squared 0.498, share 0.513). Where the model asks for 30 degrees or more the demonstrations show 1.00 (E) and 0.94 (A) of it. A plain factor of 1.25 on every twist lifts the share from 0.51 to 0.64 while the error grows from 28.0 to 29.2 degrees, so '+0.08' can be passed by bigger twists alone. Over the 23 fold runs the share follows the twist's mean size (+0.92) (c01) [measured]. Item 2's base line is also off: by metrics.json the fixed rule gives 103 right, 27 early, 8 in between, 71 never (106 and 32 are the verifier's looser count), and 24 of the 71 fire on the last picture, after which the recording has no picture left to confirm (c03 part C, c04 part B) [measured].
- The checker's fix: A panel fixed before the first run, with today's values written beside it. (1) How closely the asked twist, summed over ten rows, follows the shown one on left-out shapes (correlation, today 0.71; nine seed pairs differ by at most 0.041): keep a candidate at +0.05 with both seeds, and only if the demonstrations still show between 0.85 and 1.1 of what the model asks (today 0.96), so that bigger twists alone cannot pass. The share is reported, not judged. (2) The stop over whole episodes, with a stop-like last picture counted as a stop (today 127 right, 27 early, 8 in between, 47 never): keep at 15 more right stops with both seeds and no more early ones. (3) Guard: path within 45 degrees not down by more than 2 points with either seed. (4) Robustness candidates by their own stress tests, as the critic says. (5) One line for states off the line: direction on the first five rows after a hand correction (today 33.5 % against 69.5 % elsewhere). (6) The six course sheets, each left out with all new shapes in training, are the headline; new shapes are shown apart. Until the calibration table of session 2 shows which line follows the robot, no single line may veto a candidate that passes the others. Cost: 1 code day (c01 and c04 are a start), no robot, no GPU run.

**P3** (high). Where: Step 4, third bullet ('Whether to drop old episodes ... is decided by scores on unseen tracks'), and step 3 as a whole

- Problem: The plan records a new style and then mixes it about 1 to 4 with the old one. It names only the six square starts and the worst loop episodes for dropping. Where the two styles answer the same picture differently the model will average them, which the check already saw as tiny undecided answers.
- Evidence: Calculated, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m01_style_against_the_rules.out.txt: after round 2 the new pictures are 4,416 of 22,448 (19.7 %); when every picture counts the same the 18 shapes weigh like 6.8 shapes, and sheet 04 alone holds 27.7 %. Measured, same file: in 32 % of pushing rows (2,916 of 9,084) the puck travels more than 30 degrees off the picture's top. Measured, same folder, m02_rule_3_forced_or_chosen.out.txt: where the travel runs more than 30 degrees off the picture's top AND joint 4 has room to turn (571 rows), the recorded answer is 'push on' (0.7 cm or more) in 83.5 % and 'turn nearly in place' in 5.8 %; rule 3 asks for the second. Check results: run 6 answered 0.16 cm where look-alike demonstrations go two ways; the square start averaged four ways into 0.4 cm straight ahead. Reasoning: leaving one shape out also mixes the labels, so a model that learns the new style is marked down on the old sheets.
- The critic's fix: (1) Add columns to the training index that tag every old row by the new rules: puck travel within 30 degrees of the picture's top or not, forced by the limit or not, wrist within 3 degrees of 13.5, centred where it was measured. (2) Sample per shape, so that a shape with 2 to 4 episodes is not drowned (report 9.1 item 13 moves from 'low' to 'do'). (3) Fix three variants before any result: all old rows; only the old rows that follow the rules; old rows that break them at a weight of 0.3. Judge them on the new-shape folds with the panel of P2, and keep the six sheets as a guard because they are graded too. (4) Word rule 3 so that it continues what most old rows already do: the camera leads into the bend while pushing; stop and walk the tip round the puck only where the bend is tighter than the puck can follow (all hairpin passes already go the same way in 96 to 100 %, check, bends claim 3). Cost: 1 code day; 3 variants x 2 seeds x 18 folds at about 2 minutes each = about 3.6 GPU hours; no robot.
- Check: **partly**. m01 and m02 reproduce (m02 rerun identical; my own count in c03 part E agrees) [measured]. After round 2 the new pictures are about a fifth (19.7 % with 48 episodes of 92 pictures; a little more with the recoveries and line ends) [calculated]. The plan names only two groups of old episodes for dropping [read]. The clash with rule 3 is smaller than the objection suggests. Where the puck's travel is known (64.2 % of step rows), 69.8 % of rows already have it within 30 degrees of the picture's top, 23.3 % are further off because the limit forces it, and 6.9 % are further off with room to turn (the critic's own count: 571 rows, 5.0 %) [measured]. The larger differences between old and new are elsewhere: the start (170 of 210 episodes from one pose with the forearm straight), the wrist (within 3 degrees of 13.5 in 57 % of rows), the walk in the loops (sheets 05 and 06, a quarter of the pictures) and centring. The check supports the reworded rule 3: the twist follows the bend ahead (correlation -0.45, orientation claim 18) and hairpin passes go one way in 96 to 100 % (bends claim 3). Fix 3's variant 'only the old rows that follow the rules' is not workable as written: the travel is unknown for 36 % of rows; rows that can be shown to follow rules 3 and 6 are 34.9 % (59.8 % without the wrist rule), in a median of three separate pieces per episode; sheets 04 and 06 would keep under 30 % (c03 part E) [measured]. Per-shape sampling is sound and cheap (report 6.3: sheet 04 holds up to 44.2 % of a fold's rows).
- The checker's fix: Decide by whole episodes, not by rows. (1) Per-shape sampling becomes the default as soon as the first new shapes exist (report 9.1 item 13 moves to 'do'). (2) Tag every old episode once with what is known for the whole episode: sheet, start kind, operator, loop or not, share of rows beyond the camera's reach, median wrist, number of hand corrections. Row tags only where they exist. (3) Fix two variants before any result: all old episodes; and all but the groups that break a written rule as whole episodes (the six square-portrait starts, and the loop episodes only if the hand trial chooses a loop way other than the recorded walk). No third variant with a weight of 0.3. (4) Judge both with the panel on the six left-out course sheets and on the new-shape folds. (5) Word rule 3 as the critic says: the camera leads into the bend while pushing; turn in place only where the bend is tighter than the puck can follow. Cost: 0.5 to 1 code day; 2 variants x 2 seeds x 18 folds at about 2 minutes = about 2.4 GPU hours; no robot.

**P4** (high). Where: Step 4 candidates; step 3 table, rows 'Recoveries' and 'Line ends'; section 2.2 item 5

- Problem: The plan itself says that on an unseen line 'does it stop at all' is the bigger risk. Yet step 4 has no candidate for the stop, the new data add few stop pictures, and short recoveries would add false ones.
- Evidence: Measured (check results, stop and centring, missed 4; D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/04_runs/gpu_ki014/2026-11-10_resnet18_chunk_stop_weight_25/metrics.json): 106 right, 71 never, 32 early of 209 on unseen sheets; never on 64 % of sheet 02's and 60 % of sheet 06's episodes. Stop pictures are 259 of 18,032 rows. Calculated: the plan adds 48 whole lines and 10 to 30 line ends, that is 58 to 78 stop pictures (+22 to +30 %); by the report's own fit (+8.7 points of right stops per doubling, section 8.2) this is +2.5 to +3.3 points, 5 to 7 episodes of 209 (extrapolated, so a rough guide). Read in the code: the replay ends every episode with a stop row wherever the guiding ended (D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/02_code/record_episodes.py line 1541: proposal 'stop' after the last waypoint) and training_targets.py stop_targets (line 322) trains on every one. So 30 recoveries that end when the puck is back on the line would add 30 'stop' pictures in the middle of a line, 12 % of all stop examples, where early stops are already 32.
- The critic's fix: (a) Every recovery and every correction is guided on to the line's end, or its episode carries a flag and its stop row is left out of the stop loss (about 10 lines of code and one column). (b) Two near-end starts on every new print with the puck 0.5 to 1.5 cm aside (the stop analyst's 'end zone' episodes): about 2.4 minutes each (report 8.4) plus teaching the start, 48 episodes in about 4 hours, in place of '10, then up to 30'. By the same fit even 96 new stop pictures give only about +4 points, so the stop also needs (c): one stop candidate in step 4 with the rule the report already fixed: the stop trained on every picture within 2 cm of the end (9.1 item 8, never run end to end) and the combined rule that needs no training (item 9). (d) Print some tracks with the end far from the paper's edge (the generator has the setting): 34 of 89 such ends never stopped against 16 of 120 near ones (report 4.5). Cost: 0.25 code day; 1 to 3 robot hours more than planned; 4 GPU runs (under 1 hour).
- Check: **partly**. Read in the code: the exact replay proposes 'stop' after the last waypoint (record_episodes.py line 1540) and stop_targets (training_targets.py line 322) trains on every stop row; step 4 of the plan names no stop candidate. The cleaned data hold 259 stop rows in 209 episodes, 50 of them relabelled copies (c03 part C) [measured]. So recoveries that end in the middle of a line would add false stop pictures: that stands. The arithmetic (58 to 78 new stop pictures, +2.5 to +3.3 points by the report's fit) is right as a rough guide [calculated]; the fit is per doubling of all pictures, not of stop pictures. The size of the problem is overstated. Of the 71 episodes that never stop under 'two in a row', 40 have no stop-like picture at all, 24 fire on the last picture (no second picture exists to confirm; on the four robot runs that reached the end the second picture did confirm) and 7 fire once earlier. With the last picture counted the rule gives 127 right, 27 early, 8 in between, 47 never; sheet 02 falls from 29 to 18 'never' (c04 part B) [measured]. Fix (b), 48 near-end starts 'in place of 10, then up to 30', contradicts the critic's own keep list (the first batch of 10 with the number shown) and skips the report's gate: the check 'train once without the 42 near-end-start episodes' was never run (report 9.1 item 6 and its 'not run' list). The stop candidates of fix (c) exist in the code (stop_target 'at_goal' and 'goal_distance', training_probes.py lines 208 to 216); the report's frozen probes found the near-goal window about as good as the stop picture, so expect little. I tried a free variant, the fifth predicted position of model E as a graded distance to the end: no gain (joined with 'or', never falls from 71 to 62 while early rises from 27 to 44; c02) [measured]. The generator has the setting for ends far from the edge (make_test_tracks.py lines 143 and 144).
- The checker's fix: (1) Every recovery, correction and near-end start is guided on to the line's end, so that each adds a true stop picture; the stop rows of the few that cannot be are dropped by a rule in the training index (about 10 lines). (2) Before more than the first batch of 10 line ends is recorded, run two things on ki-014 (minutes each, 2 seeds): training without the 42 existing near-end-start episodes, and the two stop targets that exist in the code. Score both with the last picture counted. The number of further near-end starts follows from that result, and they are recorded on the new prints, not on the six sheets. (3) Some new tracks end far from the paper's edge, some in the middle of the sheet. (4) Keep question 3 to the professor (must the robot stop by itself, does a stop past the end count). Cost: 0.25 code day, about 1 GPU hour, robot time as in the plan until the two runs are read.

**P5** (high). Where: Step 5 (correction round); step 1 (tools); calendar week 7.12 to 13.12; question 6

- Problem: The correction round is described as if the tool existed. It does not, nobody owns it, it comes in the last robot week with one try, and the rules that make corrections useful are missing.
- Evidence: Read in the code: D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/02_code/model_mode.py switches the motors off only to place the start (lines 705 to 709) and has no take-over key; record_episodes.py has four deciders (keyboard, gamepad and two replays, lines 804 to 816) and none that asks a model. Report 4.6 lists both as missing. Step 1 of the plan does not list them, and the recording tool belongs to the Recording session (HOW_WE_WORK.md). Report 8.3: 50 correction episodes give about 1,600 rows, 9 % of the data, against rounds of 20 to 33 % in the studies it read; a take-over costs a median 51.6 s and replays 22 rows; the model's own stop must wait for Enter or early stops are never corrected. None of this is in the plan. Calendar: robot work ends 20.12.26, so a round in the week of 7.12 leaves one iteration.
- The critic's fix: (1) Start now with a version that needs no new code and no answer to question 6: after each failed model run, record an ordinary hand-guided episode from a taught start at the place of the failure to the line's end (the recording page has MID and near-end starts). (2) Put the real take-over into step 1 with an owner, 2 to 4 code days (a guess) and the 5-episode pilot of report 8.4 (0.4 to 0.6 robot hours). (3) Three small rounds of 15 to 20 corrections from the week of 23.11 (about 1.5 robot hours each), each followed by retraining (minutes) and 6 paired runs, in place of one round of 50. (4) A written take-over rule: as soon as the puck's centre leaves the ink, not when the puck is lost; also for 'stopped too early' and 'ran past the end'. (5) Train only on the human's part and sample so that corrections are about a quarter of each batch (from memory, not checked: weighting the interventions up is what Mandlekar et al. 2020 did). (6) The driver frozen before round 1, as the plan says. Cost: 2 to 4 code days, 4.5 to 5 robot hours (as planned), about 6 GPU runs.
- Check: **stands**. Read in the code: model_mode.py switches the motors off only in robot_run to place the start (lines 705 to 709) and ends a run on Esc only. record_episodes.py chooses between four deciders (lines 804 to 816: exact replay, replay, gamepad, keyboard); none asks a model. Step 1 of the plan lists five tools and no take-over. The recording tool belongs to the Recording and data session (HOW_WE_WORK.md). The calendar puts the one round in the week of 7.12 with robot work ending on 20.12. Report 8.3 holds the rules the plan leaves out (the model's stop must wait for Enter; train on the human's part only; 50 episodes are about 9 % of the rows) [all read]. Two parts of the fix need changing. Fix 1 ('start now': an ordinary episode from a taught start at the place of the failure) is a demonstration at a hard place, not a correction of the model's own state, and it would add old-style episodes on the six sheets before the rules are written. Fix 3's '6 paired runs' after each small round decide nothing (report 7.5: with 10 trials each the better of a 90 % and a 70 % model is picked only 87 % of the time), and rounds of 15 to 20 episodes are about 3 % of the data each, against 20 to 33 % in the studies the report read [calculated]. The 'quarter of each batch' is one more setting; the report fixed 'compare a share of 10 to 25 % with plain mixing' (6.3).
- The checker's fix: (1) The take-over goes into step 1 with an owner named by the orchestrator (Recording session), 2 to 4 code days [guess], the written take-over rule (as soon as the puck's centre leaves the ink; also for an early stop and for running past the end; the model's stop waits for Enter) and the 5-episode pilot (0.4 to 0.6 robot hours). (2) The driver is frozen as soon as decision 6 is taken after session 2, not in session 3, so that corrections can start when the tool and the professor's answer are there. (3) The same 50 episodes in two rounds of about 25, one week apart, each on the newest model, on fresh prints as well as the six sheets; retrain from scratch after each (minutes); one paired test with at least 24 pairs at the end. Short looks in between are not tests. (4) Train on the human's part only; plain mixing with per-shape sampling is the default, a larger share of corrections per batch is one fixed comparison. (5) Until the tool exists, record nothing 'at failure places' in the old style; the plan's deliberate recoveries wait for the written rules. Cost: 2 to 4 code days, 4.5 to 5 robot hours as planned, about 4 GPU runs.

**P6** (medium). Where: Step 3, rule 2 ('an episode that runs beside the line for long is recorded again') and the table row 'Recoveries'

- Problem: The rule treats centring as something to select afterwards. In this tool the pictures come from the replay and the moves from the earlier guiding pass. So a puck that drifts in the replay gets a move that was never an answer to that picture. Recording such an episode again throws away the very place where a recovery could have been recorded.
- Evidence: Read: D:/AI_Project/claude_memory/project-recording-tool.md ('guide, then replay ... only the replay is training data'; h corrects by hand, u flags a mistake). Measured (check results, stop and centring claims 8, 10 and 16; view novelty claim 17): for offsets of 7 mm or more the demonstrations shrink the offset by a median of 2.9 mm over five steps (smaller in 55 %, larger in 23 %); pushed pictures are within 7 mm in 68 % and over 12 mm in 16 %; of 365 sampled off-line pictures 245 are followed by the puck coming back; model E is more than 45 degrees off its own training labels on 23 % of moving rows. The operators pressed h 239 times in 209 episodes (report 8.3). D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/02_code/config.py TRAINING_TARGET_PARAMETERS: 'is_mistake_dropped': True, so rows flagged with u are already left out of training.
- The critic's fix: Rule 2 becomes: 'In the replay, as soon as the puck's centre leaves the ink, press h and guide it back, then go on to the end. Press u for the steps in which it drifted.' The window shows the live offset (the puck map exists; for printed tracks take the line from the track's own drawing so that it also works on dashes). An episode is recorded again only when the puck was lost. At the old rate this gives about one recovery per episode on the new shapes (about 48 in round 2) and makes the separate 30 recoveries on old sheets optional. Cost: 0.5 to 1 code day in the recording tool (Recording session, through the orchestrator); saves up to 3 robot hours.
- Check: **partly**. The mechanism is as the critic says: only the replay is training data and the moves come from the guiding pass (claude_memory/project-recording-tool.md); the check's numbers are quoted correctly (stop and centring claims 8, 10, 16); is_mistake_dropped is True (config.py line 1430) [read]. I found 238 hand corrections in 130 of the 209 episodes (c03 part D) [measured], so 'about one per episode' holds. Three limits. The u key flags only the step before (record_episodes.py line 1843), so 'u for the steps in which it drifted' needs a change to the tool or a cleaning rule. A live offset on a printed track needs the sheet's place on the table and the fitted camera, a measure with no ruler check yet that fails on dashes (plan section 3): more than half a day. And the project's numbers do not show that corrections made during a replay teach recovery. On unseen sheets model E is within 45 degrees on 33.5 % of the first five rows after a hand correction ('always forward' would score 26.1 %) against 69.5 % elsewhere (52.8 %) [measured, c03 part D]; leaving the old corrections out cost no more direction than leaving out as many other rows (report 8.3). So the 30 deliberate recoveries are not made 'optional' by this fix.
- The checker's fix: Rule 2 becomes: 'Watch the replay. When the puck's centre leaves the ink, pause, press h, guide it back and go on to the end. Record again only when the puck was lost.' By eye first; the live offset waits for the ruler check of block E. In place of many u presses, a cleaning rule drops the K rows before every hand correction, for old and new episodes alike. Test it now on the 238 old corrections (K = 3 and 5, 2 seeds, six folds: about 50 minutes on ki-014; measure: the 33.5 % above, with path within 45 degrees as guard). Offline these rows look ordinary to the model (it agrees with their labels in 65.2 % against 67.0 % overall, c04 part B), so this is a test, not a sure gain. The 30 deliberate recoveries stay in the plan until that test or a robot run shows that corrections during the replay are learnt. Cost: 0.25 code day for the rule, about 1 GPU hour, no extra robot time.

**P7** (medium). Where: Step 3, first paragraph ('tried by hand at the robot for half an hour'); calendar (first drive of a retrained model in session 3)

- Problem: About ten robot hours go into a new style before any model trained on it has driven. Whether a one-picture model can learn the style is found out in session 3, two weeks before robot work ends.
- Evidence: Plan section 5: recording from 16.11 to 6.12, session 3 in the same block. Training takes about 2 minutes per fold on ki-014 (check results, bends recommendation 4). CLAUDE.md: test before a change. Measured, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m06_is_the_style_learnable.out.txt: a base line a pilot can be read against: 0.59 of the net turn where the camera looks along the line (412 windows), 0.20 to 0.28 where it does not (87 windows).
- The critic's fix: After the hand trial record 8 episodes on 4 new shapes (about 1.5 hours with laying out). Retrain the same evening with per-shape sampling and two seeds. Decide before the rest is recorded: (a) offline, the four shapes left out in turn: net turn given back 0.59 or more, and no more answers under 0.3 cm than today (4.7 %, check, bends claim 8); (b) on the robot: 2 runs on each of 2 fresh prints, scored from the pictures. If (a) fails, change the rules, not the amount. Cost: 1.5 robot hours moved forward (not added), 20 minutes of driving, about 0.5 GPU hour.
- Check: **partly**. The calendar is as the critic says: recording runs from 16.11 to 6.12 and a retrained model first drives in session 3; a fold trains in about 2 minutes (bends recommendation 4, STATUS.md) [read]. A pilot is right. Its pass rule is not. The 0.59 is what today's models, which never saw the new style, already give wherever the camera looks along the line, so reaching it shows no learning. Six pilot episodes among 18,000 old pictures (3 %) cannot teach a model the style. On 8 episodes the number is too loose: drawing 2 whole-line episodes from each of 4 of the present sheets 2,000 times gives 0.40 to 0.62 (5th to 95th percentile, median 0.50, about 42 sharp windows), and only 9 % of draws reach 0.59 with nothing changed (c03 part F) [measured]. The measure itself rewards bigger twists (see P2). Four robot runs decide nothing either.
- The checker's fix: Keep the pilot, change what it decides. Eight episodes on 4 new prints, each print recorded once by Emranul and once by Marshal, in about 1.5 hours. Read from them, with limits written before: (1) can the rules be kept (share of pushing rows with the puck's travel within 30 degrees of the picture's top, 68 % in the old data; wrist; hand corrections per episode); (2) do two recordings of the same print agree (forearm angle and tip path at the same place along the line: 'one style' measured directly); (3) minutes per episode and per lay-out, which settles 6 hours against 9 to 10; (4) today's model on these pictures as a look, with no pass rule. If (1) or (2) fails, change the rules, not the amount. From then on the newest model drives each fresh print once before it is demonstrated, and the model is retrained every week, so the new style meets a model from the first recording day and not in session 3. Cost: 1.5 robot hours moved forward, 20 minutes of model runs, minutes of GPU.

**P8** (medium). Where: Step 3 table, row 'New shapes ... about 12 new tracks, 4 episodes each ... about 6 [robot hours]'

- Problem: Twelve shapes with four episodes each is not the best split of the same robot time, and six hours is too low.
- Evidence: Report 8.2 (measured on frozen features): +2.5 points per doubling of sheets at equal pictures (+1.6 to +3.4), +1.7 per doubling of pictures; five sheets at a quarter of their episodes beat one full sheet on all six folds. Measured, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m05_what_the_offline_rule_sees.out.txt: a shape with four episodes is read to plus or minus 5.1 points anyway (the mean of 12 such shapes to plus or minus 1.5), so repeats do not buy a readable score per shape. Hours, calculated: 5.8 minutes is the time from start to start INSIDE one sheet (check results, orientation claim 15, 192 gaps); the same claim adds 10 minutes per new lay-out (36 x 5.8 + 18 x 10 minutes = 6.5 h). For the plan's 48 episodes in 24 lay-outs: 48 x 5.8 + 24 x 10 minutes = 8.6 hours; with the report's own guess of half an hour per new track (8.4): 10.6 hours.
- The critic's fix: Record 24 shapes with 2 episodes each: one solid and one dashed print of the same shape, laid once by rule 0 (the same 24 lay-outs, the same 8.6 hours). Keep both prints of a shape, and its near-end starts, in the same fold. Before each demonstration let the current model drive the fresh print once (about 2 minutes): 24 to 48 free runs of the exam case, where session 2 has 12. Put 9 to 10 hours into the calendar for this row. Cost: no extra recording hours against the honest figure, 1 to 1.5 robot hours for the model's attempts, 0.5 code day to draw 24 tracks with the existing generator.
- Check: **partly**. The hours stand. The check's 5.8 minutes is the start-to-start time inside one sheet (orientation claim 15, 192 gaps) and the same claim counts 10 minutes per new lay-out, so 48 episodes in 24 lay-outs are 8.6 hours, and 10.6 with the report's half hour per new track [calculated]; the plan's 'about 6' is too low. The split is a judgement with the report behind its direction (8.2: +2.5 points per doubling of sheets at equal pictures) but small in size: from 18 to 30 shapes the same fit gives about +1.8 points, an extrapolation beyond five sheets and below the 3.3-point spread of single runs [calculated]. Not counted by the critic: with one episode per print nothing is ever repeated, so the main data cannot show whether the style is kept; a first episode on a brand-new shape will need more corrections and re-recordings than the 5.8 minutes assume [guess]; each shape is seen in one lay-out only. Letting the model drive each fresh print first is a good idea. But only one program may be connected to the robot at a time (CLAUDE.md), so every run means closing the recording tool and placing the start by hand: 3 to 4 minutes a run, 1.2 to 3.2 hours for 24 to 48 runs, not 1 to 1.5 [guess, from the measured 1.1 to 1.2 s per step and 1.9 minutes of set-up].
- The checker's fix: Book 9 to 10 robot hours for this row. Record 18 to 24 shapes and decide between 3 and 2 episodes per shape in the pilot (if a first episode on a new print needs more than about two hand corrections, take 3). Keep 4 of the shapes with repeats (2 lay-outs, solid and dashed) as the place where style and lay-out are measured. Solid and dashed prints of one shape, and its near-end starts, stay in one fold. Model runs on fresh prints are batched at the start of each recording session: lay each of the day's prints on its tape marks, one run each, then close the driver and open the recording tool once. Write this into the session checklist, and label the runs as looks, because model and driver change from week to week. Cost: no recording hours beyond the honest figure; 1.5 to 2.5 robot hours for the model runs; 0.5 code day for the tracks, which step 1 must redraw anyway.

**P9** (medium). Where: Step 3, rule 4 ('One way through a loop ... either the walk round the puck as recorded, or unwinding the forearm early on a straight stretch')

- Problem: Both ways need to know how much room the forearm has left, or that a loop is coming. The picture shows neither well, and the walk did not carry over between the two loops we have.
- Evidence: Measured, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m03_forearm_angle_from_the_picture.out.txt, part B: in windows where the demonstrators unwind by 30 degrees or more from |joint 4| of 60 or more (240 windows), the models that never saw the sheet give back 0.52 to 0.70 of the turn on sheets 03 and 04, where the line itself shows the way, but 0.04 (E) and 0.23 (A) on sheet 05 and 0.35 and 0.44 on sheet 06. With |joint 4| at 105 to 121 degrees the demonstrators unwind on average (-0.84 degrees per row) and model E asks -0.06. Part A (a least-squares read-out on the frozen features saved on 9.11.26, the scored sheet left out; no network trained; a floor, a fine-tuned network may read more): the picture gives the forearm's side in 97 % and its angle to a median of 17 degrees (r 0.85; on sheet 02 lying square r 0.89), but it finds only 35 % of the rows within 17 degrees of the limit at 10 % false alarms (area under the curve 0.74). So the task's premise 'the limit is not visible' is half right: the side and the rough angle are visible, the last degrees are not. Check results (dashed line claim 8): every all-sheets model asks for the walk on run 7's pictures and none on run 8's.
- The critic's fix: (1) Decide first whether loops are worth it: question 2 to the professor is already in the plan; if unseen tracks hold no loop, record no new loop style. (2) If they do: in the hand trial choose a way whose trigger the picture shows (the forearm's side and 'far turned' can be read; '17 degrees before the limit' cannot), for example 'flip when the line points straight back at the robot'; write the trigger into the rule; record it on at least 4 loop shapes in both turning senses (16 episodes, about 2.5 hours inside the 48). (3) Measure fixed in advance: unwinding windows on left-out loop shapes at 0.5 or more of the shown turn. Cost: nothing beyond the hand trial unless loops are in; then 2.5 robot hours inside round 2.
- Check: **partly**. m03 part B matches its output and the check. On windows where the demonstrators unwind the forearm, the models that never saw the sheet follow them on sheets 03 and 04 and hardly at all on sheet 05 (share 0.04 for E, 0.23 for A; the share has the weakness named under P2, but sheet 05's correlation is also the lowest of sheets 02 to 06, 0.46 for E, c01). Every all-sheets model asks for the walk on run 7's pictures and none on run 8's (dashed line claim 8); all 13 demonstrations from end A of sheet 06 unwind by 147 to 216 degrees (claim 7) [measured]. Part A I did not rerun; its output says what the critic reports (side right in 97 %, median error 17 degrees, 35 % of the rows near the limit found at 10 % false alarms). So both ways named in rule 4 need something one picture gives poorly: that stands. The fix's example does not escape it. 'Flip when the line points straight back at the robot' needs the same knowledge of where the robot is. And during a flip of about 200 degrees every picture must say 'go on' although rule 3 says 'turn back towards the line'; a model without memory needs a visible mark of the phase, which the recorded walk has (the tip leaves the puck) [reasoning]. Sixteen episodes for a new manoeuvre are few when 54 old ones on two shapes did not carry over, and a second loop way beside the 54 old loop episodes (a quarter of the pictures) is the two-styles clash of P3. Sheets 05 and 06 are loops and are graded, so loops are in whatever the professor answers.
- The checker's fix: Do not invent a new loop way. Keep the recorded walk round the puck as the one way through a loop, and write down its visible trigger from what the demonstrations of sheets 05 and 06 did at the same place (18 and 13 passes that all unwind). If the professor says unseen tracks may hold loops, spend the loop share of round 2 on more loop shapes in this way (at least 4, both turning senses), so that the walk is seen on more than two shapes. If not, record no new loop shapes and treat sheets 05 and 06 as sheets the model knows. Lower the need with the lay-out rule of P1. Measure fixed in advance: how closely the asked twist follows the shown one in unwinding windows on left-out loop shapes (sheet 05 today: correlation about 0.3 beyond the camera's reach, 0.46 overall). Cost: nothing beyond the hand trial unless loops are in; then about 2.5 robot hours inside round 2.

**P10** (medium). Where: Step 4, candidates: 'a step target that separates which way from how far' and 'the previous picture as a second input'

- Problem: The check's second cause, the model turning too little, has no candidate, and the one that looks aimed at it cannot help it. The history candidate costs much for what the probes showed.
- Evidence: Measured, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m04_unit_step.out.txt (it reproduces the verifier's 0.42 and 0.63 to 0.68): on unseen sheets the asked net turn is 0.51 (E) and 0.52 (A) of the shown one in sharp windows, whatever the step rule. Calculated from the same table: a perfect 'how far' output (travel exactly as shown) would give 0.51 to 0.52 of the shown turn per cm, less than the model's own length (0.63 to 0.68), which gains only by travelling 0.71 as far; the answer scaled to one recorded step gives 0.55 to 0.61. Measured, same folder, m07_seed_spread_of_the_turn_measure.out.txt: 23 trainings, the twist focus included (0.553 and 0.558), stay between 0.42 and 0.60. Measured, m06: what moves the turn is where the camera looks (0.59 against 0.20 to 0.28). Check results (bends claims 14, 15, 17): a names output is 8 to 12 points worse at bends and split in only 2 to 4 % of bend pictures; the previous picture gains 1.2 to 2.9 points at sharp bends on frozen features, inside the seed spread, and shows the mark of copying the last move; carrying out 2 or 3 chunk steps would twist 10 to 19 degrees without a new look.
- The critic's fix: Say in the plan that the cure for the turning is the data (P1, P3, P7) and that no recipe so far has moved it. Keep 'which way / how far' only as a way to take the scaling rule out of the driver, judged on pace and stop place, not on bends. Strike from the list: the previous picture, mixtures or classes for the direction, and carrying out several chunk steps (too much for 18,000 pictures and eight weeks). Order of offline work, each judged with the panel of P2: (1) per-shape sampling as soon as the first new shapes exist; (2) the stop candidates of P4; (3) the old-row variants of P3; (4) augmentations by their stress tests. Cost: nothing added; it removes about 2 code days (history) and question 7 to the professor.
- Check: **partly**. m04 gives the verifier's 0.42 and 0.63 to 0.68 again, and a 'which way / how far' target leaves the twist output untouched, so it cannot change the turn [measured; reasoning]. That part stands. So do the costs named for a second picture at bends (+1.2 to +2.9 points on frozen features, bends claim 17), for a names output (8 to 12 points worse, claim 15) and for carrying out several predicted steps (10 to 19 degrees of twist without a new look, claim 14). Three readings do not hold. (1) 'The model turns too little' is not a bias waiting for a cure: where the model asks for 30 degrees or more, the demonstrations show 1.00 (E) and 0.94 (A) of it; what is low is how closely it follows the demonstrations (correlation 0.71) (c01) [measured]. (2) 'No recipe moved it in 23 trainings' says nothing about other targets: the 23 are one family (one picture, the same kind of twist output; network, loss weights, augmentation and number of predicted steps vary), and their share follows the twist's mean size (+0.92). (3) 'What moves the turn is where the camera looks' compares inside reach with beyond the limit (277 of 326 'off' windows are forced, 30 are free), so it supports a lay-out rule, not rule 3 against pushing on where there is room (c03 part B). Striking question 7 saves nothing, because a question costs nothing, and the same probe gains 6 to 10 points at the line's end (0.436 against 0.497, 0.530, 0.538, bends claim 17), where one picture is blind for the last 2.5 cm.
- The checker's fix: Say in the plan: 'on an unseen sheet the model's twist follows the demonstrations only loosely (correlation 0.71 over ten rows); where it asks for a sharp turn it asks the right amount; nothing is to be scaled up'. Keep the critic's order of offline work (per-shape sampling, stop candidates, old-episode variants, augmentations by their stress tests), judged with the panel of P2 as corrected. Keep 'which way / how far' only to take the scaling rule out of the driver, judged on pace and stop place. Keep question 7 in the letter; if the answer is yes, a second picture is a late, optional experiment for the line's end only, after the copy test of report 5.4. Mixtures, direction classes and carrying out several predicted steps stay out. Cost: nothing added.

**P11** (medium). Where: Step 2, block A, and decision 6 ('The driver's step rule: decided by blocks A and B'); section 8, second bullet (the end)

- Problem: Two of block A's three settings sit at the same point of the only dial there is, the travel per decision. And the stop place is not part of the decision, although the step rule moves it.
- Evidence: Measured, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m04_unit_step.out.txt: share of the shown turn per cm in sharp windows on unseen sheets: fixed 1.0 cm 0.42; own length 0.63 (E) and 0.68 (A); fixed 0.65 cm 0.64 and 0.65; the verifier's step_length/verify/v20 gives 0.83 to 0.85 for a fixed 0.5 cm. Decisions per cm: 1.0, about 1.5 and 1.54. A step scaled to one recorded step (every recorded step has the size 1 by the '1 cm or 5 degrees' rule: m01, 5th percentile 1.02, median 1.23) keeps the pace (1.08 to 1.15 times the demonstrations' rows) and a full last step, but only 0.55 to 0.61 of the turn per cm. So no scaling rule gives both, and I cannot name a better principled rule than a measured one. The end: at the first picture with a stop chance of 0.5 the own length carries out a median of 0.38 cm (E; 10th to 90th percentile 0.23 to 0.55) or 0.29 cm (A), where the driver of 11.11 moved 1.0 cm. The shown last move is itself a left-over of the waypoint rule (median 0.65 cm, under 0.5 cm in 37 %, m01), so the short answers near the end copy book-keeping, not intent. Block A's runs end with Esc after the bend and block B has no pass rule.
- The critic's fix: Run block A as three well separated fixed steps, 1.0, 0.65 and 0.45 cm, 4 short runs each (the same 12 runs; step_m is one setting). Take the LARGEST step that passes 3 of 4, because time is the second criterion. The own length joins in block B against the fixed step of the same mean. Add to decision 6: a step rule is taken on only if in block B's full runs the stop place, by the measure checked in block E, is at the median no more than 3 mm shorter than with the driver of 11.11; otherwise the last move after the first stop picture stays one full centimetre as a rule of its own, named in question 5 to the professor. Cost: no extra runs, no code, two lines in the checklist.
- Check: **partly**. The numbers are right (m04; the verifier's v20 gives 0.83 and 0.85 for a fixed 0.5 cm) [measured]; for a fixed step the share is plain arithmetic, 0.42 divided by the step in cm. That the model's own length and a fixed 0.65 cm sit at the same pace is known to the plan and wanted: the verifier asked for exactly this control ('without it a robot win of the own length cannot be told from a win of shorter steps', step length, missed 4). Dropping the own length from block A removes that comparison at the one bend it was designed for. A third, shorter fixed step is a real gain. 'Take the largest step that passes 3 of 4' buys time, the second criterion, with finishing, the first, on four runs (3 of 4 fits any true rate from about 0.30 to 0.95) [calculated], against the plan's own 'time is tuned last'. The 3 mm rule on the stop cannot be read: block B has three runs per setting and does not contain the driver of 11.11, stops scatter by 4 to 6 mm (stop and centring, challenge 3) and the measure's own tolerance in block E is 3 mm. What stands: decision 6 names blocks A and B only, block B has no pass rule, and the step rule moves the stop (m04: 0.38 cm carried out at the first stop-like picture against 1.0 cm) [measured]. Not seen by the critic: block A uses model E, which was trained on sheet 03, so the step rule would be chosen on one bend of a known sheet.
- The checker's fix: Block A with four settings, 3 to 4 short runs each: fixed 1.0, fixed 0.65, the model's own length, fixed 0.45 cm (4 more short runs, about 10 minutes). Use the model trained without sheet 03 (the checkpoint exists) and add the tightest bend of one fresh print, so that the rule is chosen in the exam case and not on a known sheet. Score each run by the puck's largest offset through the bend (a number) beside pass or fail; choose by margin and tune time last. The end gets its own short block instead of a rule on three stops: near-end starts 6 to 8 cm before the end, 8 runs per step rule on two sheets (about 25 minutes), read with the photos of block E. A rule of its own for the end (a full last centimetre) is taken on only if the own length stops short there by more than the measure's error, and is then named in question 5. Add to decision 6: 'and the end block'. Cost: about 35 robot minutes more, no code beyond the switches of step 1.

**P12** (medium). Where: Step 3, rules 5 and 6

- Problem: Rule 6 cannot be kept by a hand that guides an arm with all motors off. Rule 5 asks for something the camera does not show at the moment of stopping.
- Evidence: Measured (check results, view novelty claim 15): only 54.9 % of the training pictures have wrist and hand within 3 degrees of the values the driver holds; dashed line claim 10: on sheet 06 from end A the wrist's median is 22 to 28 degrees in the second half. Report 4.6: with the wrist held at one value the picture's centre moved more than 20 px in 23.3 % of rows. The end (check results, stop and centring claims 5 and 7): ink ahead of the puck shows in 3 of 175 stop pictures; the camera hides 13 to 15 mm beyond the puck; a miss of about 1 cm is hidden and is made sideways two or three pictures earlier.
- The critic's fix: Rule 6 becomes 'the replay holds the wrist': the recording tool replays the guided tip path and forearm twist through the driver's own solver (solve_with_wrist_fixed in D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/02_code/side_view_test.py), so every new picture is one the driver can reach. Try it on 5 test episodes first, to see whether the puck still follows. Rule 5 becomes: 'be on the middle of the line 3 cm before the end, then push straight until the end is covered'; an end counts only at 7 mm or less by the measure, after the ruler check. Cost: 0.5 to 1 code day in record_episodes.py (Recording session) and 30 robot minutes; nothing for rule 5.
- Check: **stands**. Quoted correctly: 54.9 % of training pictures have wrist and hand within 3 degrees of the held values (view novelty claim 15; my count 55.2 %, the wrist alone 56.9 %, c03 part E); on sheet 06 from end A the wrist's median is 22 to 28 degrees in the second half (dashed line claim 10); ink ahead of the puck shows in 3 of 175 stop pictures and the camera hides 13 to 15 mm beyond the puck (stop and centring claims 5 and 7) [measured]. 'Cannot be kept by hand' is a guess; what is measured is that it was not kept when nobody tried. The solver exists (side_view_test.py line 57) and the driver and the gamepad mode already use it (model_mode.py lines 285 and 321, record_episodes.py line 1515); the exact replay visits recorded joint waypoints (__exact_waypoints), so the change is contained [read]. Its cost is understated: it touches the core of a tool another session owns, it changes the rod's lean and so how the block meets the puck, and it needs a rule for waypoints the solver refuses; and four fifths of the pictures stay as they are. Rule 5's rewording matches the stop analyst's recommendation 4.
- The checker's fix: As the critic says, in this order. (1) In the hand trial, 10 minutes with the wrist angle shown live, to learn whether the hand can keep 13.5 degrees within 3 at all. (2) If not, the replay re-solves each waypoint with wrist and hand as the driver holds them (through the orchestrator to the Recording session; 1 to 2 code days with tests and a rule for refused waypoints), tried on 5 test episodes, with the count of hand corrections compared with and without. (3) Kept only if the puck still follows. Rule 5: 'be on the middle of the line 3 cm before the end, then push straight until the end is covered'; an end counts at 7 mm or less by the measure, after the ruler check. Mention in question 5 that the replay, like the driver, holds the wrist. Cost: 1 to 2 code days, 30 to 40 robot minutes.

**P13** (low). Where: Step 4, last bullet ('The pictures of the ten runs of 11.11 stay as a fixed test of their own')

- Problem: These pictures have no demonstrated answer, and the plan gives no rule for passing the test.
- Evidence: The runs are model runs: D:/AI_Project/03_data/model_runs/2026-11-11_17*/steps.csv hold the model's answers only. The check results hold the expectations: sheet 03's first bend, the demonstrations turn 96 degrees (90 to 110) where E asked 61, 74 and 61 (step length, missed 5); run 6, pictures 8 to 11, the nearest demonstrations did +47, +40, +31, +31 degrees where E asked +8, +1, -11, -19 (view novelty claim 3); run 8, pictures 27 to 40, the demonstrations unwind 119 degrees (91 to 166) where E asked 2.6 (dashed line claim 7); run 8, picture 40, a false stop on blank paper at 0.94 (dashed line claim 11); run 3, straight ahead where the line left 53 degrees to the left (orientation claim 1).
- The critic's fix: Write about eight such expectations, each with a limit, into one script and one table. Every new model gets 'k of 8' next to the panel of P2. The pictures are never trained on. Cost: 0.5 code day.
- Check: **stands**. The ten runs are model runs; their steps.csv hold the model's answers only, and the plan gives the fixed test no pass rule [read]. Four of the five expectations the critic names are quoted correctly and have demonstrations behind them: sheet 03's first bend (96 degrees, 90 to 110, against 61, 74, 61; step length, missed 5), run 6 pictures 8 to 11 (view novelty claim 3), run 8 pictures 27 to 40 (dashed line claim 7) and the false stop at picture 40 (claim 11). The fifth does not: for run 3 no demonstration shows a block touching the puck with the line leaving 53 degrees to the left. The six recorded square starts go round the puck first (orientation claim 1), so 'the right answer' there would be our own invention.
- The checker's fix: Write expectations only for pictures where the demonstrations at the same place agree (about six of the eight), each with its limit, in one script and one table. Leave run 3 out. Report 'k of n' beside the panel for every new model, never as a keep rule: all these pictures come from the six sheets and from a model that knew them, and a gate on them would tune to the six sheets, which Emranul does not want. Cost: 0.5 code day.

**P14** (low). Where: Step 4, candidate 'coloured patches outside the paper'; question 9 (table and light on the exam day)

- Problem: The picture tells a model roughly where the forearm stands, and nobody knows from what. If it is the room, patches outside the paper remove the cue and a new table changes it.
- Evidence: Measured, D:/AI_Project/.claude/worktrees/training-analysis/06_deliverables/research_notes/Model and training/evidence/robot_test_2026-11-11/plan_review/method/m03_forearm_angle_from_the_picture.out.txt, part A: r 0.85 and the side right in 97 % from frozen features with the scored sheet left out. What carries it (the robot's base coming into view, the table's perspective, the paper's edges, things in the room) was not separated: open. Guess: a loop manoeuvre that leans on the room will not carry over to the exam table.
- The critic's fix: One to two hours on the laptop, no network trained: repeat the read-out with everything outside the paper painted grey, and again with the robot's base painted grey (features recomputed by a forward pass only). Report which cue carries the angle before the patch augmentation is chosen, and judge the patch candidate also on the unwinding windows of P9. Cost: 1 to 2 hours, no robot.
- Check: **partly**. m03 part A's output says what the critic reports (frozen features, the scored sheet left out: r 0.85, side right in 97 %); I did not rerun the fit [the critic's measurement]. What carries the cue is open, and the consequence is the critic's own guess. It matters only if a manoeuvre is to lean on the forearm's angle (P9). The stated 1 to 2 hours leave out that 'everything outside the paper painted grey' needs a paper mask for 18,032 pictures; the patch candidate of step 4 needs the same mask.
- The checker's fix: No separate study. When the paper mask for the patch candidate is built, run the read-out once with the outside painted grey and once with the robot's own parts painted grey (forward passes only, half a day with the mask). Judge the patch candidate on the unwinding windows and on the folds of sheets 05 and 06 as well as on its stress test. If the cue lies in the robot's own parts it travels to any table; if it lies in the room, say so in question 9 and in the risks table. Cost: half a day, no robot.

### Missing from the plan, by the critic

1. A calibration table: for every robot run from session 2 on, the same model's offline numbers on the same shape beside the robot's result, so that the team learns which offline number follows the robot. The earlier report planned it (sections 7.2 and 7.4); the plan dropped it.
2. Tags on every new episode: version of the demonstration rules, lay-out, print (solid or dashed), operator, centimetres beyond the camera's reach, number of h corrections. Without them none of the old-against-new variants can be trained or scored.
3. How old and new data are weighted in training (per shape, per style). After round 2 the new shapes are 19.7 % of the pictures and sheet 04 alone 27.7 % (calculated, m01).
4. A check that both operators produce the same style: both record the same two shapes in the pilot, and twist per row, share of steps in place and the puck's offset are compared.
5. Written rules for a crossing (straight across) and for dashes (drive exactly as on a solid line). The seven rules cover neither.
6. The line of a printed track taken from the track's own drawing for the centring measure, so that the measure works on dashed prints. Today it fails on sheet 06 (check results, stop and centring, missed 6).
7. Folds by shape: the solid print, the dashed print, the near-end starts and the recoveries of one shape must be left out together, or the left-out shape is not unseen.
8. What the robot should do when it has lost the line. Run 8 stopped on blank paper (stop chance 0.94); no rule and no data say 'lost', and such a stop hides behind 'stopped by itself'.
9. A time budget. Time is the second exam criterion, and the step rules cost 1.4 to 2 times the decisions (measured, m04: 1.39 to 1.45 times the demonstrations' rows for the own length or a fixed 0.65 cm). The plan sets no limit in seconds per track for the moment the pace is tuned.
10. The recording method's limit for 'the puck stays in the middle': the moves come from the guiding pass and the pictures from the replay. The plan never says so, and it decides what rule 2 can achieve (see P6).
11. A statement that 23 trainings did not move the net turn (0.42 to 0.60, m07), so that no further recipe search is started in the hope of curing the bends.

### The same list, checked

1. Calibration table (offline numbers beside the robot's result for the same model and shape): really missing; the report planned it (7.2), the plan has only the scoring command. Matters: it is the only way to learn which offline number follows the robot. Cost: one table per session.
2. Tags on every new episode: partly missing. The index already holds operator, plan number, start kind, start end, sheet orientation, hand corrections, lighting and camera mount [read in the cleaned rows' columns]. Missing: the rules' version, the print (solid or dashed), centimetres beyond the camera's reach. Matters, costs little.
3. How old and new data are weighted: really missing. Matters: sheet 04 holds up to 44.2 % of a fold's rows today (report 6.3) and the new shapes would be about a fifth of the pictures.
4. A check that both operators produce the same style: really missing, and it matters more than the critic says: 16,802 of the 18,032 cleaned rows (93 %) are Emranul's and 1,230 Marshal's [measured], so a second operator doing a large share of round 2 is itself a change of style.
5. Written rules for a crossing and for dashes: really missing from the seven rules. Matters (sheets 05 and 06 have a crossing; the dashes are the open cause). Cost: two sentences and a try in the hand trial.
6. The line taken from the track's own drawing for the centring measure: really missing. Matters, because half of the new prints are dashed and the measure fails on sheet 06. It needs the sheet's place on the table (tape marks), so it is more than a line of code.
7. Folds by shape: half there ('one shape left out at a time'). What must be written: the solid print of sheet 06's shape and the dashed print of sheet 05's shape belong to the folds of sheets 06 and 05, and recoveries and line ends go with their sheet. Matters.
8. What the robot does when it has lost the line: not really missing. The plan ends such runs (stall rule, step budget) and its stop measure fails a stop that is not on the end, so run 8's false stop counts as a failure. A 'lost' output has no data behind it and is not needed.
9. A time budget: missing, but it waits for the professor (question 3 asks for the limit) and the plan tunes time last on purpose. Worth one line: the short step rules cost 1.39 to 1.45 times the demonstrations' rows (m04) [measured].
10. The recording method's limit said plainly (moves from the guiding pass, pictures from the replay): really missing. Matters: it decides what rule 2 can achieve (see P6).
11. A statement that 23 trainings did not move the net turn: should not go in as worded. The 23 are one family and the number follows the twist's size (+0.92, c01). The true sentence is: the model asks the right amount where it asks for a turn, and is unsure where the sharp turns come (correlation 0.71).

### Missed by the critic

1. The plan's cause 2, 'the model itself turns too little' (2.2 item 2), rests on a measure that is below one by construction, and the critic built on it instead of testing it. Windows picked by what the demonstration did give 0.51 (E) and 0.52 (A); windows picked by what the model asks (30 degrees or more) show the demonstrations doing 1.00 and 0.94 of the asked turn; the best single factor on the twist is about 1 (0.96); a factor of 1.5 lifts the share to 0.77 and the error from 28 to 32 degrees [measured, c01]. So the model is honest but unsure (correlation 0.71), and anything that only makes twists bigger would look like progress. Fix: reword cause 2, judge by the correlation and by 'what the model asks, the demonstrations show', and keep the plan's 'no twist factor'. Cost: 0.5 code day (c01 is a start), no robot.
2. The stop base line, 'never in 71 of 209', is partly an artefact of scoring recordings with 'two pictures in a row': 24 of the 71 fire on the last picture, after which the recording has no picture left; 40 have no stop-like picture at all; 7 fire once earlier [measured, c03 part C]. With the last picture counted: 127 right, 27 early, 8 in between, 47 never (c04 part B). The recordings also hold no picture taken after the stop, so what a model says one step past the end is untested. Fix: correct the scoring rule before any stop candidate is compared (else a candidate that fires one picture earlier wins by the rule alone), and let the end block of session 2 show what the robot sees one step past the end. Cost: 0.25 code day, about 25 robot minutes.
3. Neither the plan nor the critic's panel has a line for states off the line, although those are what a robot that drives by itself produces and the data already hold them: 238 hand corrections in 130 episodes. On unseen sheets model E is within 45 degrees on 33.5 % of the first five rows after a correction ('always forward': 26.1 %) against 69.5 % elsewhere (52.8 %) [measured, c03 part D]. The report listed this measure (7.2, measure 4). It is today's best offline sign of whether the robot can recover, and it questions the hope that more of the same corrections will teach recovery. Fix: add the line to the panel, and hold back one in four of the new recoveries and corrections, by shape, as a test set that is never trained on. Cost: 0.25 code day, no robot.
4. 'Unseen' in the plan means 'another draw from our own track generator': new shapes, development tracks and the sealed set all come from make_test_tracks.py, and the tracks of 10.11 were too easy (smallest radius 3.5 cm against 0.8 to 2.2 on the sheets) [read]. The only tracks drawn by the professor are the six sheets and the extra files promised on 26.10. Fix: make 'each course sheet left out, with all new shapes in training' the headline number (it shows directly whether our shapes help on the professor's shapes, at no extra cost); keep at least half of the professor's extra tracks sealed as a final test instead of training on all of them; before printing, match the generator to measured features of the six sheets (tightest radius, length, loop, crossing, dash and gap lengths, the end's distance from the paper's edge). Cost: 0.5 code day; no robot hours beyond the plan.
5. Rule 1 swaps one exact start for another, and the critic's rule 0 adds one favourable lay-out; Emranul asked for 'any orientation' and the exam start is placed by hand. 170 of 210 recordings start from one pose, and the ten runs began 1.3 to 5.0 cm away with the forearm turned by up to 10 degrees [measured, bends claim 20]. A lay-out rule cannot buy 'any orientation' either: the camera turns about 110 degrees to each side, and the best placement by the critic's own rule still leaves 15.7 % of the six sheets' line beyond its reach (20.0 % as recorded) [calculated, c04 part A]. Fix: write a tolerance band into rule 1 (gap 4 to 6 cm, up to 1 cm to the side, forearm within 10 degrees of the line) and spread the new starts over it on purpose; in every robot session run a small labelled block of lay-outs that were never recorded; and put to Emranul, as one decision in plain words, what 'any orientation' can mean with this camera. Cost: no extra recording time; about 20 robot minutes per session.

### To keep

1. Step 0: the questions to the professor sent now and in writing, and the decisions put to Emranul one at a time.
2. Session 2's design: tape marks and the controlled start; a control for every question; rules written before the session; alternating order; every run scored from its pictures after the run and outside the driver; the photo and ruler check (block E); the dry run of small steps (block 0).
3. Block B (the exam case: the model that never saw sheet 03 on sheet 03, and model E on new prints) and block C (a solid print of sheet 06's shape, a dashed print of sheet 05's shape).
4. More different tracks before more episodes, with bends as tight as the course sheets and each shape printed solid and dashed. Report 8.2 supports it, and m07 adds that 23 recipes did not move the turn, so the data are the lever.
5. One written style, tried by hand first, used by both operators. The intent of rule 3 (the camera looks along the line) has the project's own numbers behind it: 0.59 of the net turn given back where the camera looks along the line, 0.20 to 0.28 where it does not (measured, m06). It also serves Emranul's wish for any orientation, because the picture then hardly depends on how the sheet lies.
6. Rule 1's start with a gap of about 5 cm between tip and puck (the recordings: median 5.5 cm; the ten runs: 2.3 to 3.6 cm).
7. The first batch of 10 line ends with the number shown, more only if they come out better.
8. Every score on tracks the model was not trained on, two seeds, rules fixed before the result, and a sealed set that nobody looks at before the final test.
9. The fixed test from the 11.11 pictures and the dash check on run 7's pictures, with pass rules added (P13).
10. Section 8's list of what will not be done: no hand-written steering, no twist factor as the fix, no switch of model, no extra push while the driver moves 1 cm. m04 agrees: every scaling rule lands between 0.42 and 0.68 of the demonstrated turn per cm, none restores it.
11. Step 5's order: driver frozen before corrections, training from scratch on everything, a paired test of new against old, a second round only if the first helped, model and driver frozen by 13.12.26.
12. No promised percentages before session 2 has measured where the robot stands, and nothing judged on the six sheets alone.
